LG1867	1	STARTING from fish-shape Paumanok, where I was born,
LG1867	2	Well-begotten, and rais'd by a perfect mother;
LG1867	3	After roaming many lands—lover of populous pave-mentspavements;
LG1867	4	Dweller in Mannahatta, city of ships, my city—or on southern savannas;
LG1867	5	Or a soldier camp'd, or carrying my knapsack and gun —or a miner in California;
LG1867	6	Or rude in my home in Dakotah's woods, my diet meat, my drink from the spring;
LG1867	7	Or withdrawn to muse and meditate in some deep recess,
LG1867	8	Far from the clank of crowds, intervals passing, rapt and happy;
LG1867	9	Aware of the fresh free giver, the flowing Missouri— aware of mighty Niagara;
LG1867	10	Aware of the buffalo herds, grazing the plains—the hirsute and strong-breasted bull;
LG1867	11	Of earths, rocks, Fifth-month flowers, experienced— stars, rain, snow, my amaze;
LG1867	12	Having studied the mocking-bird's tones, and the mountain hawk's,
LG1867	13	And heard at dusk the unrival'd one, the hermit thrush from the swamp-cedars,
LG1867	14	Solitary, singing in the West, I strike up for a New World.
LG1867	15	Victory, union, faith, identity, time,
LG1867	16	Yourself, the present and future lands, the indissolu-bleindissoluble compacts, riches, mystery,
LG1867	17	Eternal progress, the kosmos, and the modern reports.
LG1867	18	This, then, is life;
LG1867	19	Here is what has come to the surface after so many throes and convulsions.
LG1867	20	How curious! how real!
LG1867	21	Under foot the divine soil—over head the sun.
LG1867	22	See, revolving, the globe;
LG1867	23	The ancestor-continents, away, group'd together;
LG1867	24	The present and future continents, north and south,with the isthmus between.
LG1867	25	See, vast, trackless spaces;
LG1867	26	As in a dream, they change, they swiftly fill;
LG1867	27	Countless masses debouch upon them;
LG1867	28	They are now cover'd with the foremost people, arts,institutions, known.
LG1867	29	See, projected, through time,
LG1867	30	For me, an audience interminable.
LG1867	31	With firm and regular step they wend—they never stop,
LG1867	32	Successions of men, Americanos, a hundred millions;
LG1867	33	One generation playing its part, and passing on,
LG1867	34	Another generation playing its part, and passing on in its turn,
LG1867	35	With faces turn'd sideways or backward towards me,to listen,
LG1867	36	With eyes retrospective towards me.
LG1867	37	Americanos! Conquerors! marches humanitarian;
LG1867	38	Foremost! century marches! Libertad! masses!
LG1867	39	For you a programme of chants.
LG1867	40	Chants of the prairies;
LG1867	41	Chants of the long-running Mississippi, and down to the Mexican sea;
LG1867	42	Chants of Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, Iowa, Wisconsin and Minnesota;
LG1867	43	Chants going forth from the centre, from Kansas, and thence, equi-distant,
LG1867	44	Shooting in pulses of fire, ceaseless, to vivify all.
LG1867	45	In the Year 80 of The States,
LG1867	46	My tongue, every atom of my blood, form'd from this soil, this air,
LG1867	47	Born here of parents born here, from parents the same,and their parents the same,
LG1867	48	I, now thirty-six years old, in perfect health, begin,
LG1867	49	Hoping to cease not till death.
LG1867	50	Creeds and schools in abeyance,
LG1867	51	(Retiring back a while, sufficed at what they are, but never forgotten,)
LG1867	52	I harbor, for good or bad—I permit to speak, at every hazard,
LG1867	53	Nature now without check, with original energy.
LG1867	54	Take my leaves, America! take them South, and take them North!
LG1867	55	Make welcome for them everywhere, for they are your own offspring;
LG1867	56	Surround them, East and West! for they would sur-roundsurround you;
LG1867	57	And you precedents! connect lovingly with them, for they connect lovingly with you.
LG1867	58	I conn'd old times;
LG1867	59	I sat studying at the feet of the great masters:
LG1867	60	Now, if eligible, O that the great masters might re-turnreturn and study me!
LG1867	61	In the name of These States, shall I scorn the antique?
LG1867	62	Why these are the children of the antique, to jus-tifyjustify it.
LG1867	63	Dead poets, philosophs, priests,
LG1867	64	Martyrs, artists, inventors, governments long since,
LG1867	65	Language-shapers, on other shores,
LG1867	66	Nations once powerful, now reduced, withdrawn, or desolate,
LG1867	67	I dare not proceed till I respectfully credit what you have left, wafted hither :
LG1867	68	I have perused it—own it is admirable, (moving awhile among it;)
LG1867	69	Think nothing can ever be greater—nothing can ever deserve more than it deserves;
LG1867	70	Regarding it all intently a long while, then dismiss-ingdismissing it,
LG1867	71	I stand in my place, with my own day, here.
LG1867	72	Here lands female and male;
LG1867	73	Here the heirship and heiress-ship of the world—here the flame of materials;
LG1867	74	Here Spirituality, the translatress, the openly-avow'd,
LG1867	75	The ever-tending, the finale of visible forms;
LG1867	76	The satisfier, after due long-waiting, now advancing,
LG1867	77	Yes, here comes my mistress, the Soul.
LG1867	78	The SOUL!
LG1867	79	Forever and forever—longer than soil is brown and solid—longer than water ebbs and flows.
LG1867	80	I will make the poems of materials, for I think they are to be the most spiritual poems;
LG1867	81	And I will make the poems of my body and of mor-talitymortality,
LG1867	82	For I think I shall then supply myself with the poems of my Soul, and of immortality.
LG1867	83	I will make a song for These States, that no one State may under any circumstances be sub-jectedsubjected to another State;
LG1867	84	And I will make a song that there shall be comity by day and by night between all The States, and between any two of them;
LG1867	85	And I will make a song for the ears of the President,full of weapons with menacing points,
LG1867	86	And behind the weapons countless dissatisfied faces :
LG1867	87	And a song make I, of the One form'd out of all;
LG1867	88	The fang'd and glittering One whose head is over all;
LG1867	89	Resolute, warlike One, including and over all;
LG1867	90	(However high the head of any else, that head is over all.)
LG1867	91	I will acknowledge contemporary lands;
LG1867	92	I will trail the whole geography of the globe, and sa-lutesalute courteously every city large and small;
LG1867	93	And employments! I will put in my poems, that with you is heroism, upon land and sea—And I will report all heroism from an American point of view;
LG1867	94	And sexual organs and acts! do you concentrate in me—for I am determin'd to tell you with cour-ageouscourageous clear voice, to prove you illustrious.
LG1867	95	I will sing the song of companionship;
LG1867	96	I will show what alone must finally compact These;
LG1867	97	I believe These are to found their own ideal of manly love, indicating it in me;
LG1867	98	I will therefore let flame from me the burning fires that were threatening to consume me;
LG1867	99	I will lift what has too long kept down those smoul-deringsmouldering fires;
LG1867	100	I will give them complete abandonment;
LG1867	101	I will write the evangel-poem of comrades and of
LG1867	102	love;
LG1867	103	(For who but I should understand love, with all its
LG1867	104	sorrow and joy?
LG1867	105	And who but I should be the poet of comrades?)
LG1867	106	I am the credulous man of qualities, ages, races;
LG1867	107	I advance from the people en-masse in their own spirit;
LG1867	108	Here is what sings unrestricted faith.
LG1867	109	Omnes! Omnes! let others ignore what they may;
LG1867	110	I make the poem of evil also—I commemorate that part also;
LG1867	111	I am myself just as much evil as good, and my nation is—And I say there is in fact no evil,
LG1867	112	(Or if there is, I say it is just as important to you, to the land, or to me, as anything else.)
LG1867	113	I too, following many, and follow'd by many, inau-gurateinaugurate a Religion—I too go to the wars;
LG1867	114	(It may be I am destin'd to utter the loudest cries thereof, the winner's pealing shouts;
LG1867	115	Who knows? they may rise from me yet, and soar above every thing.)
LG1867	116	Each is not for its own sake;
LG1867	117	I say the whole earth, and all the stars in the sky, are for Religion's sake.
LG1867	118	I say no man has ever yet been half devout enough
LG1867	119	None has ever yet adored or worship'd half enough;
LG1867	120	None has begun to think how divine he himself is, and how certain the future is.
LG1867	121	I say that the real and permanent grandeur of These States must be their religion;
LG1867	122	Otherwise there is no real and permanent grandeur;
LG1867	123	(Nor character, nor life worthy the name, without Re-ligionReligion;
LG1867	124	Nor land, nor man or woman, without Religion.)
LG1867	125	What are you doing, young man?
LG1867	126	Are you so earnest—so given up to literature, science,art, amours?
LG1867	127	These ostensible realities, politics, points?
LG1867	128	Your ambition or business, whatever it may be?
LG1867	129	It is well—Against such I say not a word—I am their poet also;
LG1867	130	But behold! such swiftly subside—burnt up for Re-ligionReligion's sake;
LG1867	131	For not all matter is fuel to heat, impalpable flame,the essential life of the earth,
LG1867	132	Any more than such are to Religion.
LG1867	133	What do you seek, so pensive and silent?
LG1867	134	What do you need, Camerado?
LG1867	135	Dear son! do you think it is love?
LG1867	136	Listen, dear son—listen, America, daughter or son!
LG1867	137	It is a painful thing to love a man or woman to ex-cessexcess—and yet it satisfies—it is great;
LG1867	138	But there is something else very great—it makes the whole coincide;
LG1867	139	It, magnificent, beyond materials, with continuous hands, sweeps and provides for all.
LG1867	140	Know you! to drop in the earth the germs of a greater Religion,
LG1867	141	The following chants, each for its kind, I sing.
LG1867	142	My comrade!
LG1867	143	For you, to share with me, two greatnesses—and a third one, rising inclusive and more resplen-dentresplendent,
LG1867	144	The greatness of Love and Democracy—and the greatness of Religion.
LG1867	145	Melange mine own! the unseen and the seen;
LG1867	146	Mysterious ocean where the streams empty;
LG1867	147	Prophetic spirit of materials shifting and flickering around me;
LG1867	148	Living beings, identities, now doubtless near us, in
LG1867	149	the air, that we know not of;
LG1867	150	Contact daily and hourly that will not release me;
LG1867	151	These selecting—these, in hints, demanded of me.
LG1867	152	Not he, with a daily kiss, onward from childhood kissing me,
LG1867	153	Has winded and twisted around me that which holds me to him,
LG1867	154	Any more than I am held to the heavens, to the spir-itualspiritual world,
LG1867	155	And to the identities of the Gods, my lovers, faithful and true,
LG1867	156	After what they have done to me, suggesting themes.
LG1867	157	O such themes! Equalities!
LG1867	158	O amazement of things! O divine average!
LG1867	159	O warblings under the sun—usher'd, as now, or at noon, or setting!
LG1867	160	O strain, musical, flowing through ages—now reach-ingreaching hither,
LG1867	161	I take to your reckless and composite chords—I add to them, and cheerfully pass them forward.
LG1867	162	As I have walk'd in Alabama my morning walk,
LG1867	163	I have seen where the she-bird, the mocking-bird on her nest in the briers, hatching her brood.
LG1867	164	I have seen the he-bird also;
LG1867	165	I have paused to hear him, near at hand, inflating his throat, and joyfully singing.
LG1867	166	And while I paused, it came to me that what he really sang for was not there only,
LG1867	167	Nor for his mate nor himself only, nor all sent back by the echoes;
LG1867	168	But subtle, clandestine, away beyond,
LG1867	169	A charge transmitted, and gift occult, for those being born.
LG1867	170	Democracy!
LG1867	171	Near at hand to you a throat is now inflating itself
LG1867	172	and joyfully singing.
LG1867	173	Ma femme!
LG1867	174	For the brood beyond us and of us,
LG1867	175	For those who belong here, and those to come,
LG1867	176	I, exultant, to be ready for them, will now shake out carols stronger and haughtier than have ever yet been heard upon earth.
LG1867	177	I will make the songs of passion, to give them their way,
LG1867	178	And your songs, outlaw'd offenders—for I scan you with kindred eyes, and carry you with me the same as any.
LG1867	179	I will make the true poem of riches,
LG1867	180	To earn for the body and the mind, whatever adheres,and goes forward, and is not dropt by death.
LG1867	181	I will effuse egotism, and show it underlying all— and I will be the bard of personality;
LG1867	182	And I will show of male and female that either is but the equal of the other;
LG1867	183	And I will show that there is no imperfection in the present—and can be none in the future;
LG1867	184	And I will show that whatever happens to anybody, it may be turn'd to beautiful results—and I will show that nothing can happen more beautiful than death;
LG1867	185	And I will thread a thread through my poems that time and events are compact,
LG1867	186	And that all the things of the universe are perfect miracles, each as profound as any.
LG1867	187	I will not make poems with reference to parts;
LG1867	188	But I will make leaves, poems, poemets, songs, says,thoughts, with reference to ensemble:
LG1867	189	And I will not sing with reference to a day, but with reference to all days;
LG1867	190	And I will not make a poem, nor the least part of a poem, but has reference to the Soul;
LG1867	191	(Because, having look'd at the objects of the universe,I find there is no one, nor any particle of one,but has reference to the Soul.)
LG1867	192	Was somebody asking to see the Soul?
LG1867	193	See! your own shape and countenance—persons, sub-stancessubstances, beasts, the trees, the running rivers, the rocks and sands. All hold spiritual joys, and afterwards loosen them:
LG1867	194	How can the real body ever die, and be buried?
LG1867	195	Of your real body, and any man's or woman's real body,
LG1867	196	Item for item, it will elude the hands of the corpse-cleanerscorpse-cleaners, and pass to fitting spheres,
LG1867	197	Carrying what has accrued to it from the moment of birth to the moment of death.
LG1867	198	Not the types set up by the printer return their im-pressionimpression, the meaning, the main concern,
LG1867	199	Any more than a man's substance and life, or a wo-manwoman's substance and life, return in the body and the Soul,
LG1867	200	Indifferently before death and after death.
LG1867	201	Behold! the body includes and is the meaning, the main concern—and includes and is the Soul;
LG1867	202	Whoever you are! how superb and how divine is your body, or any part of it.
LG1867	203	Whoever you are! to you endless announcements.
LG1867	204	Daughter of the lands, did you wait for your poet?
LG1867	205	Did you wait for one with a flowing mouth and in-dicativeindicative hand?
LG1867	206	Toward the male of The States, and toward the female of The States,
LG1867	207	Live words—words to the lands.
LG1867	208	O the lands! interlink'd, food-yielding lands!
LG1867	209	Land of coal and iron! Land of gold! Lands of cotton, sugar, rice!
LG1867	210	Land of wheat, beef, pork! Land of wool and hemp!Land of the apple and grape!
LG1867	211	Land of the pastoral plains, the grass-fields of the world! Land of those sweet-air'd interminable plateaus!
LG1867	212	Land of the herd, the garden, the healthy house of adobieadobe!
LG1867	213	Lands where the northwest Columbia winds, and where the southwest Colorado winds!
LG1867	214	Land of the eastern Chesapeake! Land of the Dela-wareDelaware!
LG1867	215	Land of Ontario, Erie, Huron, Michigan!
LG1867	216	Land of the Old Thirteen! Massachusetts land! Land of Vermont and Connecticut!
LG1867	217	Land of the ocean shores! Land of sierras and peaks!
LG1867	218	Land of boatmen and sailors! Fishermen's land!
LG1867	219	Inextricable lands! the clutch'd together! the passion-atepassionate ones!
LG1867	220	The side by side! the elder and younger brothers!the bony-limb'd!
LG1867	221	The great women's land! the feminine! the ex-periencedexperienced sisters and the inexperienced sisters!
LG1867	222	Far breath'd land! Arctic braced! Mexican breez'd!the diverse! the compact!
LG1867	223	The Pennsylvanian! the Virginian! the double Caro-linianCarolinian!
LG1867	224	O all and each well-loved by me! my intrepid nations!O I at any rate include you all with perfect love!
LG1867	225	I cannot be discharged from you! not from one, any sooner than another!
LG1867	226	O Death! O for all that, I am yet of you, unseen, this hour, with irrepressible love,
LG1867	227	Walking New England, a friend, a traveler,
LG1867	228	Splashing my bare feet in the edge of the summer ripples, on Paumanok's sands,
LG1867	229	Crossing the prairies—dwelling again in Chicago— dwelling in every town,
LG1867	230	Observing shows, births, improvements, structures,arts,
LG1867	231	Listening to the orators and the oratresses in public halls,
LG1867	232	Of and through The States, as during life—each man and woman my neighbor,
LG1867	233	The Louisianian, the Georgian, as near to me, and I as near to him and her,
LG1867	234	The Mississippian and Arkansian yet with me—and I yet with any of them;
LG1867	235	Yet upon the plains west of the spinal river—yet in my house of adobieadobe,
LG1867	236	Yet returning eastward—yet in the Sea-Side State, or in Maryland,
LG1867	237	Yet Kanadian, cheerily braving the winter—the snow and ice welcome to me,
LG1867	238	Yet a true son either of Maine, or of the Granite State,or of the Narragansett Bay State, or of the Empire State;
LG1867	239	Yet sailing to other shores to annex the same—yet welcoming every new brother;
LG1867	240	Hereby applying these leaves to the new ones, from the hour they unite with the old ones;
LG1867	241	Coming among the new ones myself, to be their com-panioncompanion and equal—coming personally to you now;
LG1867	242	Enjoining you to acts, characters, spectacles, with me.
LG1867	243	With me, with firm holding—yet haste, haste on.
LG1867	244	For your life, adhere tome;
LG1867	245	Of all the men of the earth, I only can unloose you and toughen you;
LG1867	246	I may have to be persuaded many times before I consent to give myself to you—but what of that?
LG1867	247	Must not Nature be persuaded many times?
LG1867	248	No dainty dolce affettuoso I;
LG1867	249	Bearded, sunburnt, gray-neck'd, forbidding, I have arrived,
LG1867	250	To be wrestled with as I pass, for the solid prizes of the universe;
LG1867	251	For such I afford whoever can persevere to win them.
LG1867	252	On my way a moment I pause;
LG1867	253	Here for you! and here for America!
LG1867	254	Still the Present I raise aloft—Still the Future of The States I harbinge, glad and sublime;
LG1867	255	And for the Past, I pronounce what the air holds of &
LG1867	256	The red aborigines!
LG1867	257	Leaving natural breaths, sounds of rain and winds,calls as of birds and animals in the woods,syllabled to us for names;
LG1867	258	Okonee, Koosa, Ottawa, Monongahela, Sauk, Natchez,Chattahoochee, Kaqueta, Oronoco,
LG1867	259	Wabash, Miami, Saginaw, Chippewa, Oshkosh, Walla-WallaWalla-Walla;
LG1867	260	Leaving such to The States, they melt, they depart,charging the water and the land with names.
LG1867	261	O expanding and swift! O henceforth,
LG1867	262	Elements, breeds, adjustments, turbulent, quick, and audacious;
LG1867	263	A world primal again—Vistas of glory, incessant and branching;
LG1867	264	A new race, dominating previous ones, and grander far, with new contests,
LG1867	265	New politics, new literatures and religions, new in-ventionsinventions and arts.
LG1867	266	These! my voice announcing—I will sleep no more,but arise;
LG1867	267	You oceans that have been calm within me! how I feel you, fathomless, stirring, preparing un-precedentedunprecedented waves and storms.
LG1867	268	See! steamers steaming through my poems!
LG1867	269	See, in my poems immigrants continually coming and landing;
LG1867	270	See, in arriere, the wigwam, the trail, the hunter's hut, the flat-boat, the maize-leaf, the claim, the rude fence, and the backwoods village;
LG1867	271	See, on the one side the Western Sea, and on the other the Eastern Sea, how they advance and retreat upon my poems, as upon their own shores;
LG1867	272	See, pastures and forests in my poems—See, animals,wild and tame—See, beyond the Kanzas, count-lesscountless herds of buffalo, feeding on short curly grass;
LG1867	273	See, in my poems, cities, solid, vast, inland, with paved streets, with iron and stone edifices,ceaseless vehicles, and commerce;
LG1867	274	See, the many-cylinder'd steam printing-press—See,the electric telegraph, stretching across the Continent, from the Western Sea to Man-hattanManhattan;
LG1867	275	See, through Atlantica's depths, pulses American,Europe reaching—pulses of Europe, duly re-turnreturn'd;
LG1867	276	See, the strong and quick locomotive, as it departs,panting, blowing the steam-whistle;
LG1867	277	See, ploughmen, ploughing farms—See, miners, dig-gingdigging mines—See, the numberless factories;
LG1867	278	See, mechanics, busy at their benches, with tools— See from among them, superior judges, philo-sophsphilosophs, Presidents, emerge, drest in working dresses;
LG1867	279	See, lounging through the shops and fields of The States, me, well-beloved, close-held by day and night;
LG1867	280	Hear the loud echoes of my songs there! Read the hints come at last.
LG1867	281	O Camerado close!
LG1867	282	O you and me at last—and us two only.
LG1867	283	O a word to clear one's path ahead endlessly!
LG1867	284	O something extatic and undemonstrable! O music wild!
LG1867	285	O now I triumph—and you shall also;
LG1867	286	O hand in hand—O wholesome pleasure—O one more desirer and lover!
LG1867	287	O to haste, firm holding—to haste, haste on, with me.
LG1867	288	I CELEBRATE myself;
LG1867	289	And what I assume you shall assume;
LG1867	290	For every atom belonging to me, as good belongs to you.
LG1867	291	I loafe and invite my Soul;
LG1867	292	I lean and loafe at my ease, observing a spear of sum-mersummer grass.
LG1867	293	Houses and rooms are full of perfumes—the shelves are crowded with perfumes;
LG1867	294	I breathe the fragrance myself, and know it and like it;
LG1867	295	The distillation would intoxicate me also, but I shall not let it.
LG1867	296	The atmosphere is not a perfume—it has no taste of the distillation—it is odorless;
LG1867	297	It is for my mouth forever—I am in love with it;
LG1867	298	I will go to the bank by the wood, and become undis-guisedundisguised and naked;
LG1867	299	I am mad for it to be in contact with me.
LG1867	300	The smoke of my own breath;
LG1867	301	Echoes, ripples, buzz'd whispers, love-root, silk-thread,crotch and vine;
LG1867	302	My respiration and inspiration, the beating of my heart, the passing of blood and air through my lungs;
LG1867	303	The sniff of green leaves and dry leaves, and of the shore, and dark-color'd sea-rocks, and of hay in the barn;
LG1867	304	The sound of the belch'd words of my voice, words loos'd to the eddies of the wind;
LG1867	305	A few light kisses, a few embraces, a reaching around of arms;
LG1867	306	The play of shine and shade on the trees as the supple boughs wag;
LG1867	307	The delight alone, or in the rush of the streets, or along the fields and hill-sides;
LG1867	308	The feeling of health, the full noon trill, the song of me rising from bed and meeting the sun.
LG1867	309	Have you reckon'd a thousand acres much? have you reckon'd the earth much?
LG1867	310	Have you practis'd so long to learn to read?
LG1867	311	Have you felt so proud to get at the meaning of poems?
LG1867	312	Stop this day and night with me, and you shall pos-sesspossess the origin of all poems;
LG1867	313	You shall possess the good of the earth and sun— (there are millions of suns left;)
LG1867	314	You shall no longer take things at second or third hand, nor look through the eyes of the dead,nor feed on the spectres in books;
LG1867	315	You shall not look through my eyes either, nor take things from me:
LG1867	316	You shall listen to all sides, and filter them from your-selfyour-self.
LG1867	317	I have heard what the talkers were talking, the talk of the beginning and the end.
LG1867	318	But I do not talk of the beginning or the end.
LG1867	319	There was never any more inception than there is now,
LG1867	320	Nor any more youth or age than there is now;
LG1867	321	And will never be any more perfection than there is now,
LG1867	322	Nor any more heaven or hell than there is now.
LG1867	323	Urge, and urge, and urge;
LG1867	324	Always the procreant urge of the world.
LG1867	325	Out of the dimness opposite equals advance—always substance and increase, always sex;
LG1867	326	Always a knit of identity—always distinction—always a breed of life.
LG1867	327	To elaborate is no avail—learn'd and unlearn'd feel that it is so.
LG1867	328	Sure as the most certain sure, plumb in the uprights,well entretied, braced in the beams,
LG1867	329	Stout as a horse, affectionate, haughty, electrical,
LG1867	330	I and this mystery, here we stand.
LG1867	331	Clear and sweet is my Soul, and clear and sweet is all that is not my Soul.
LG1867	332	Lack one lacks both, and the unseen is proved by the seen,
LG1867	333	Till that becomes unseen, and receives proof in its turn.
LG1867	334	Showing the best, and dividing it from the worst,age vexes age;
LG1867	335	Knowing the perfect fitness and equanimity of things,while they discuss I am silent, and go bathe and admire myself.
LG1867	336	Welcome is every organ and attribute of me, and of any man hearty and clean;
LG1867	337	Not an inch, nor a particle of an inch, is vile, and none shall be less familiar than the rest.
LG1867	338	I am satisfied—I see, dance, laugh, sing;
LG1867	339	As the hugging and loving Bed-fellow sleeps at my side through the night, and withdraws at the peep of the day, with stealthy tread,
LG1867	340	Leaving me baskets cover'd with white towels, swell-ingswelling the house with their plenty,
LG1867	341	Shall I postpone my acceptation and realization, and scream at my eyes,
LG1867	342	That they turn from gazing after and down the road,
LG1867	343	And forthwith cipher and show me a cent,
LG1867	344	Exactly the contents of one, and exactly the contents of two, and which is ahead?
LG1867	345	Trippers and askers surround me;
LG1867	346	People I meet—the effect upon me of my early life, or the ward and city I live in, or the nation,
LG1867	347	The latest dates, discoveries, inventions, societies,authors old and new,
LG1867	348	My dinner, dress, associates, looks, compliments, dues,
LG1867	349	The real or fancied indifference of some man or woman I love,
LG1867	350	The sickness of one of my folks, or of myself, or ill-doingill-doing, or loss or lack of money, or depressions or exaltations;
LG1867	351	Battles, the horrors of fratricidal war, the fever of doubtful news, the fitful events;
LG1867	352	These come to me days and nights, and go from me again,
LG1867	353	But they are not the Me myself.
LG1867	354	Apart from the pulling and hauling stands what I am;
LG1867	355	Stands amused, complacent, compassionating, idle,unitary;
LG1867	356	Looks down, is erect, or bends an arm on an impalpa-bleimpalpable certain rest,
LG1867	357	Looking with side-curved head, curious what will come next;
LG1867	358	Both in and out of the game, and watching and won-deringwondering at it.
LG1867	359	Backward I see in my own days where I sweated through fog with linguists and contenders;
LG1867	360	I have no mockings or arguments—I witness and wait.
LG1867	361	I believe in you, my Soul—the other I am must not abase itself to you;
LG1867	362	And you must not be abased to the other.
LG1867	363	Loafe with me on the grass—loose the stop from your throat;
LG1867	364	Not words, not music or rhyme I want—not custom or lecture, not even the best;
LG1867	365	Only the lull I like, the hum of your valved voice.
LG1867	366	I mind how once we lay, such a transparent sum-mersummer morning;
LG1867	367	How you settled your head athwart my hips, and gently turn'd over upon me,
LG1867	368	And parted the shirt from my bosom-bone, and plunged your tongue to my bare-stript heart,
LG1867	369	And reach'd till you felt my beard, and reach'd till you held my feet.
LG1867	370	Swiftly arose and spread around me the peace and knowledge that pass all the argument of the earth;
LG1867	371	And I know that the hand of God is the promise of my own;
LG1867	372	And I know that the spirit of God is the brother of my own;
LG1867	373	And that all the men ever born are also my brothers,and the women my sisters and lovers;
LG1867	374	And that a kelson of the creation is love;
LG1867	375	And limitless are leaves, stiff or drooping in the fields;
LG1867	376	And brown ants in the little wells beneath them;
LG1867	377	And mossy scabs of the worm-fence, and heap'd stones,elder, mullen and pokeweed.
LG1867	378	A child said, What is the grass? fetching it to me with full hands;
LG1867	379	How could I answer the child? I do not know what it is, any more than he.
LG1867	380	I guess it must be the flag of my disposition, out of hopeful green stuff woven.
LG1867	381	Or I guess it is the handkerchief of the Lord,
LG1867	382	A scented gift and remembrancer, designedly dropt,
LG1867	383	Bearing the owner's name someway in the corners,that we may see and remark, and say, Whose?
LG1867	384	Or I guess the grass is itself a child, the produced babe of the vegetation.
LG1867	385	Or I guess it is a uniform hieroglyphic;
LG1867	386	And it means, Sprouting alike in broad zones and narrow zones.
LG1867	387	Growing among black folks as among white;
LG1867	388	Kanuck, Tuckahoe, Congressman, Cuff, I give them the same, I receive them the same.
LG1867	389	And now it seems to me the beautiful uncut hair of graves.
LG1867	390	Tenderly will I use you,curling grass;
LG1867	391	It may be you transpire from the breasts of young men;
LG1867	392	It may be if I had known them I would have loved them.
LG1867	393	It may be you are from old people, and from women,and from offspring taken soon out of their mothers' laps;
LG1867	394	And here you are the mothers' laps.
LG1867	395	This grass is very dark to be from the white heads of old mothers;
LG1867	396	Darker than the colorless beards of old men;
LG1867	397	Dark to come from under the faint red roofs of mouths.
LG1867	398	O I perceive after all so many uttering tongues!
LG1867	399	And I perceive they do not come from the roofs of mouths for nothing.
LG1867	400	I wish I could translate the hints about the dead young men and women,
LG1867	401	And the hints about old men and mothers, and the offspring taken soon out of their laps.
LG1867	402	What do you think has become of the young and old men?
LG1867	403	And what do you think has become of the women and children?
LG1867	404	They are alive and well somewhere;
LG1867	405	The smallest sprout shows there is really no death;
LG1867	406	And if ever there was, it led forward life, and does not wait at the end to arrest it,
LG1867	407	And ceas'd the moment life appear'd.
LG1867	408	All goes onward and outward—nothing collapses;
LG1867	409	And to die is different from what any one supposed,and luckier.
LG1867	410	Has any one supposed it lucky to be born?
LG1867	411	I hasten to inform him or her, it is just as lucky to die, and I know it.
LG1867	412	I pass death with the dying, and birth with the new wash'd babe, and am not contain'd between my-hatmy hat and boots;
LG1867	413	And peruse manifold objects, no two alike, and every one good;
LG1867	414	The earth good, and the stars good, and their adjuncts all good.
LG1867	415	I am not an earth, nor an adjunct of an earth;
LG1867	416	I am the mate and companion of people, all just as immortal and fathomless as myself;
LG1867	417	(They do not know how immortal, but I know.)
LG1867	418	Every kind for itself and its own—for me mine, male and female;
LG1867	419	For me those that have been boys, and that love women;
LG1867	420	For me the man that is proud, and feels how it stings to be slighted;
LG1867	421	For me the sweetheart and the old maid—for me mothers, and the mothers of mothers;
LG1867	422	For me lips that have smiled, eyes that have shed tears;
LG1867	423	For me children, and the begetters of children.
LG1867	424	Undrape! you are not guilty to me, nor stale, nor discarded;
LG1867	425	I see through the broadcloth and gingham, whether or no;
LG1867	426	And am around, tenacious, acquisitive, tireless, and cannot be shaken away.
LG1867	427	The little one sleeps in its cradle;
LG1867	428	I lift the gauze, and look a long time, and silently brush away flies with my hand.
LG1867	429	The youngster and the red-faced girl turn aside up the bushy hill;
LG1867	430	I peeringly view them from the top.
LG1867	431	The suicide sprawls on the bloody floor of the bedroom;
LG1867	432	I witness the corpse with its dabbled hair—I note where the pistol has fallen.
LG1867	433	The blab of the pave, the tires of carts, sluff of boot-soles, talk of the promenaders;
LG1867	434	The heavy omnibus, the driver with his interrogating thumb, the clank of the shod horses on the granite floor;
LG1867	435	The snow-sleighs, the clinking, shouted jokes, pelts of snow-balls;
LG1867	436	The hurrahs for popular favorites, the fury of rous'd mobs;
LG1867	437	The flap of the curtain'd litter, a sick man inside,borne to the hospital;
LG1867	438	The meeting of enemies, the sudden oath, the blows and fall;
LG1867	439	The excited crowd, the policeman with his star,quickly working his passage to the centre of the crowd;
LG1867	440	The impassive stones that receive and return so many echoes;
LG1867	441	What groans of over-fed or half-starv'd who fall sun-struck, or in fits;
LG1867	442	What exclamations of women taken suddenly, who hurry home and give birth to babes;
LG1867	443	What living and buried speech is always vibrating here —what howls restrain'd by decorum,
LG1867	444	Arrests of criminals, slights, adulterous offers made,acceptances, rejections with convex lips;
LG1867	445	I mind them or the show or resonance of them—I come, and I depart.
LG1867	446	The big doors of the country-barn stand open and ready;
LG1867	447	The dried grass of the harvest-time loads the slow-drawnslow-drawn wagon;
LG1867	448	The clear light plays on the brown gray and green in-terintertinged tinged;
LG1867	449	The armfuls are packt to the sagging mow.
LG1867	450	I am there—I help—I came stretcht atop of the load;
LG1867	451	I felt its soft jolts—one leg reclined on the other;
LG1867	452	I jump from the cross-beams and seize the clover and timothy,
LG1867	453	And roll head over heels, and tangle my hair full of wisps.
LG1867	454	Alone, far in the wilds and mountains, I hunt,
LG1867	455	Wandering, amazed at my own lightness and glee;
LG1867	456	In the late afternoon choosing a safe spot to pass the night,
LG1867	457	Kindling a fire and broiling the fresh kill'd game;
LG1867	458	Falling asleep on the gather'd leaves, with my dog and gun by my side.
LG1867	459	The Yankee clipper is under her three sky-sails— she cuts the sparkle and scud;
LG1867	460	My eyes settle the land—I bend at her prow, or shout joyously from the deck.
LG1867	461	The boatman and clam-diggers arose early and stopt for me;
LG1867	462	I tuck'd my trowser-ends in my boots, and went and had a good time:
LG1867	463	You should have been with us that day round the chowder-kettle.
LG1867	464	I saw the marriage of the trapper in the open air in the far-west—the bride was a red girl;
LG1867	465	Her father and his friends sat near, cross-legged and dumbly smoking—they had moccasins to their feet, and large thick blankets hanging from their shoulders;
LG1867	466	On a bank lounged the trapper—he was drest mostly in skins—his luxuriant beard and curls pro-tectedprotect his neck—he held his bride by the hand;
LG1867	467	She had long eye-lashes—her head was bare—her coarse straight locks descended upon her volup-tuousvoluptuous limbs and reach'd to her feet.
LG1867	468	The runaway slave came to my house and stopt out-sideoutside;
LG1867	469	I heard his motions crackling the twigs of the wood-pilewoodpile;
LG1867	470	Through the swung half-door of the kitchen I saw him limpsy and weak,
LG1867	471	And went where he sat on a log, and led him in and assured him,
LG1867	472	And brought water, and fill'd a tub for his sweated body and bruis'd feet,
LG1867	473	And gave him a room that enter'd from my own, and gave him some coarse clean clothes,
LG1867	474	And remember perfectly well his revolving eyes and his awkwardness,
LG1867	475	And remember putting plasters on the galls of his neck and ankles;
LG1867	476	He staid with me a week before he was recuperated and pass'd north;
LG1867	477	(I had him sit next me at table—my fire-lock lean'd in the corner.)
LG1867	478	Twenty-eight young men bathe by the shore;
LG1867	479	Twenty-eight young men, and all so friendly:
LG1867	480	Twenty-eight years of womanly life, and all so lone-somelonesome.
LG1867	481	She owns the fine house by the rise of the bank;
LG1867	482	She hides, handsome and richly drest, aft the blinds of the window.
LG1867	483	Which of the young men does she like the best?
LG1867	484	Ah, the homeliest of them is beautiful to her.
LG1867	485	Where are you off to, lady?for I see you;
LG1867	486	You splash in the water there, yet stay stock still in your room.
LG1867	487	Dancing and laughing along the beach came the twenty-ninth bather;
LG1867	488	The rest did not see her, but she saw them and loved them.
LG1867	489	The beards of the young men glisten'd with wet, it ran from their long hair;
LG1867	490	Little streams pass'd all over their bodies.
LG1867	491	An unseen hand also pass'd over their bodies;
LG1867	492	It descended tremblingly from their temples and ribs.
LG1867	493	The young men float on their backs—their white bellies bulge to the sun—they do not ask who seizes fast to them;
LG1867	494	They do not know who puffs and declines with pen-dantpendant and bending arch;
LG1867	495	They do not think whom they souse with spray.
LG1867	496	The butcher-boy puts off his killing clothes, or sharpens his knife at the stall in the market;
LG1867	497	I loiter, enjoying his repartee and his shuffle and break-down.
LG1867	498	Blacksmiths with grimed and hairy chests environ the anvil;
LG1867	499	Each has his main-sledge—they are all out—(there is a great heat in the fire.)
LG1867	500	From the cinder-strew'd threshold I follow their movements;
LG1867	501	The lithe sheer of their waists plays even with their massive arms;
LG1867	502	Overhand the hammers swing—overhand so slow— overhand so sure:
LG1867	503	They do not hasten—each man hits in his place.
LG1867	504	The negro holds firmly the reins of his four horses —the block swags underneath on its tied-over chain;
LG1867	505	The negro that drives the dray of the stone-yard— steady and tall he stands, poised on one leg on the string-piece;
LG1867	506	His blue shirt exposes his ample neck and breast, and loosens over his hip-band;
LG1867	507	His glance is calm and commanding—he tosses the slouch of his hat away from his forehead;
LG1867	508	The sun falls on his crispy hair and moustache—falls on the black of his polish'd and perfect limbs.
LG1867	509	I behold the picturesque giant, and love him—and I do not stop there;
LG1867	510	I go with the team also.
LG1867	511	In me the caresser of life wherever moving—back-wardbackward as well as forward slueing;
LG1867	512	To niches aside and junior bending.
LG1867	513	Oxen that rattle the yoke and chain, or halt in the leafy shade! what is that you express in your eyes?
LG1867	514	It seems to me more than all the print I have read in my life.
LG1867	515	My tread scares the wood-drake and wood-duck, on my distant and day-long ramble;
LG1867	516	I believe in those wing'd purposes,
LG1867	517	And acknowledge red, yellow, white, playing within me,
LG1867	518	And consider green and violet, and the tufted crown,intentional,
LG1867	519	And do not call the tortoise unworthy because she is not something else;
LG1867	520	And the jay in the woods never studied the gamut,yet trills pretty well to me;
LG1867	521	And the look of the bay mare shames silliness out of me.
LG1867	522	The wild gander leads his flock through the cool night;
LG1867	523	Ya-honk! he says, and sounds it down to me like an invitation;
LG1867	524	(The pert may suppose it meaningless, but I listen close;
LG1867	525	I find its purpose and place up there toward the wintry sky.)
LG1867	526	The sharp hoof'd moose of the north, the cat on the house-sill, the chickadee, the prairie-dog,
LG1867	527	The litter of the grunting sow as they tug at her teats,
LG1867	528	The brood of the turkey-hen, and she with her half-spread wings;
LG1867	529	I see in them and myself the same old law.
LG1867	530	The press of my foot to the earth springs a hundred affections;
LG1867	531	They scorn the best I can do to relate them.
LG1867	532	I am enamour'd of growing outdoors,
LG1867	533	Of men that live among cattle, or taste of the ocean or woods,
LG1867	534	Of the builders and steerers of ships, and the wielders of axes and mauls, and the drivers of horses;
LG1867	535	I can eat and sleep with them week in and week out.
LG1867	536	What is commonest, cheapest, nearest, easiest, is Me;
LG1867	537	Me going in for my chances, spending for vast returns;
LG1867	538	Adorning myself to bestow myself on the first that will take me;
LG1867	539	Not asking the sky to come down to my good will;
LG1867	540	Scattering it freely forever.
LG1867	541	The pure contralto sings in the organ loft;
LG1867	542	The carpenter dresses his plank—the tongue of his foreplane whistles its wild ascending lisp;
LG1867	543	The married and unmarried children ride home to their Thanksgiving dinner;
LG1867	544	The pilot seizes the king-pin—he heaves down with a strong arm;
LG1867	545	The mate stands braced in the whale-boat—lance and harpoon are ready;
LG1867	546	The duck-shooter walks by silent and cautious stretches;
LG1867	547	The deacons are ordain'd with cross'd hands at the altar;
LG1867	548	The spinning-girl retreats and advances to the hum of the big wheel;
LG1867	549	The farmer stops by the bars, as he walks on a First-dayFirst-day loafe, and looks at the oats and rye;
LG1867	550	The lunatic is carried at last to the asylum, a confirm'd case,
LG1867	551	(He will never sleep any more as he did in the cot in his mother's bed-room;)
LG1867	552	The jour printer with gray head and gaunt jaws works at his case,
LG1867	553	He turns his quid of tobacco, while his eyes blurr with the manuscript;
LG1867	554	The malform'd limbs are tied to the surgeon's table,
LG1867	555	What is removed drops horribly in a pail;
LG1867	556	The quadroon girl is sold at the stand—the drunkard.nods by the bar-room stove;
LG1867	557	The machinist rolls up his sleeves—the policeman travels his beat—the gate-keeper marks who pass;
LG1867	558	The young fellow drives the express-wagon—(I love him, though I do not know him;)
LG1867	559	The half-breed straps on his light boots to compete in the race;
LG1867	560	The western turkey-shooting draws old and young— some lean on their rifles, some sit on logs,
LG1867	561	Out from the crowd steps the marksman, takes his position, levels his piece;
LG1867	562	The groups of newly-come immigrants cover the wharf or levee;
LG1867	563	As the woolly-pates hoe in the sugar-field, the overseer views them from his saddle;
LG1867	564	The bugle calls in the ball-room, the gentlemen run for their partners, the dancers bow to each other;
LG1867	565	The youth lies awake in the cedar-roof'd garret, and harks to the musical rain;
LG1867	566	The Wolverine sets traps on the creek that helps fill the Huron;
LG1867	567	The squaw, wrapt in her yellow-hemm'd cloth, is offering moccasins and bead-bags for sale;
LG1867	568	The connoisseur peers along the exhibition-gallery with half-shut eyes bent side-ways;
LG1867	569	As the deck-hands make fast the steamboat, the plank is thrown for the shore-going passengers;
LG1867	570	The young sister holds out the skein, while the elder sister winds it off in a ball, and stops now and then for the knots;
LG1867	571	The one-year wife is recovering and happy, having a week ago borne her first child;
LG1867	572	The clean-hair'd Yankee girl works with her sewing-machinesewing-machine, or in the factory or mill;
LG1867	573	The nine months' gone is in the parturition chamber,her faintness and pains are advancing;
LG1867	574	The paving-man leans on his two handed rammer— the reporter's lead flies swiftly over the note-book —the sign-painter is lettering with red and gold;
LG1867	575	The canal boy trots on the tow path—the bookkeeper counts at his desk, the shoemaker waxes his thread;
LG1867	576	The conductor beats time for the band, and all the performers follow him;
LG1867	577	The child is baptized—the convert is making his first professions;
LG1867	578	The regatta is spread on the bay—the race is begun —how the white sails sparkle!
LG1867	579	The drover, watching his drove, sings out to them that would stray;
LG1867	580	The pedler sweats with his pack on his back, (the pur-chaserpurchaser higgling about the odd cent;)
LG1867	581	The camera and plate are prepared, the lady must sit for her daguerreotype;
LG1867	582	The bride unrumples her white dress, the minute-hand of the clock moves slowly;
LG1867	583	The opium-eater reclines with rigid head and just-openjust-open'd lips;
LG1867	584	The prostitute draggles her shawl, her bonnet bobs on her tipsy and pimpled neck;
LG1867	585	The crowd laugh at her blackguard oaths, the men jeer and wink to each other;
LG1867	586	(Miserable! I do not laugh at your oaths, nor jeer you;)
LG1867	587	The President, holding a cabinet council, is surrounded by the Great Secretaries;
LG1867	588	On the piazza walk three matrons stately and friendly with twined arms;
LG1867	589	The crew of the fish-smack pack repeated layers of halibut in the hold;
LG1867	590	The Missourian crosses the plains, toting his wares and his cattle;
LG1867	591	As the fare-collector goes through the train, he gives notice by the jingling of loose change;
LG1867	592	The floor-men are laying the floor—the tinners are tinning the roof—the masons are calling for mortar;
LG1867	593	In single file, each shouldering his hod, pass onward the laborers;
LG1867	594	Seasons pursuing each other, the indescribable crowd is gather'd—it is the Fourth of Seventh-month —(What salutes of cannon and small arms!)
LG1867	595	Seasons pursuing each other, the plougher ploughs, the mower mows, and the winter-grain falls in the ground;
LG1867	596	Off on the lakes the pike-fisher watches and waits by the hole in the frozen surface;
LG1867	597	The stumps stand thick round the clearing, the squatter strikes deep with his axe;
LG1867	598	Flatboatmen make fast, towards dusk, near the cotton-woodcottonwood or pekan-trees;
LG1867	599	Coon-seekers go through the regions of the Red river,or through those drain'd by the Tennessee, or through those of the Arkansaw;
LG1867	600	Torches shine in the dark that hangs on the Chatta-hoocheChattahooche or Altamahaw;
LG1867	601	Patriarchs sit at supper with sons and grandsons and great-grandsons around them;
LG1867	602	In walls of adobieadobe, in canvas tents, rest hunters and trappers after their day's sport;
LG1867	603	The city sleeps, and the country sleeps;
LG1867	604	The living sleep for their time, the dead sleep for their time;
LG1867	605	The old husband sleeps by his wife, and the young husband sleeps by his wife;
LG1867	606	And these one and all tend inward to me, and I tend outward to them;
LG1867	607	And such as it is to be of these, more or less, I am.
LG1867	608	I am of old and young, of the foolish as much as the wise;
LG1867	609	Regardless of others, ever regardful of others,
LG1867	610	Maternal as well as paternal, a child as well as a man,
LG1867	611	Stuff'd with the stuff that is coarse, and stuff'd with the stuff that is fine;
LG1867	612	One of the great nation, the nation of many nations,the smallest the same, and the largest the same;
LG1867	613	A southerner soon as a northerner—a planter non-chalantnonchalant and hospitable, down by the Oconee I live;
LG1867	614	A Yankee, bound my own way, ready for trade, my joints the limberest joints on earth, and the sternest joints on earth;
LG1867	615	A Kentuckian, walking the vale of the Elkhorn in my deer-skin leggings—a Louisianian or Georgian;
LG1867	616	A boatman over lakes or bays, or along coasts—a Hoosier, Badger, Buckeye;
LG1867	617	At home on Kanadian snow-shoes, or up in the bush,or with fishermen off Newfoundland;
LG1867	618	At home in the fleet of ice-boats, sailing with the rest and tacking;
LG1867	619	At home on the hills of Vermont, or in the woods of Maine, or the Texan ranch;
LG1867	620	Comrade of Californians—comrade of free north-west-ernersnorth-westerners, (loving their big proportions;)
LG1867	621	Comrade of raftsmen and coalmen—comrade of all who shake hands and welcome to drink and meat;
LG1867	622	A learner with the simplest, a teacher of the thought-fullestthoughtfullest;
LG1867	623	A novice beginning, yet experient of myriads of sea-sonsseasons;
LG1867	624	Of every hue and caste am I, of every rank and re-ligionreligion;
LG1867	625	A farmer, mechanic, artist, gentleman, sailor, quaker;
LG1867	626	A prisoner, fancy-man, rowdy, lawyer, physician,priest.
LG1867	627	I resist anything better than my own diversity;
LG1867	628	I breathe the air, but leave plenty after me,
LG1867	629	And am not stuck up, and am in my place.
LG1867	630	(The moth and the fish-eggs are in their place;
LG1867	631	The suns I see, and the suns I cannot see, are in their place;
LG1867	632	The palpable is in its place, and the impalpable is in its place.)
LG1867	633	These are the thoughts of all men in all ages and lands—they are not original with me;
LG1867	634	If they are not yours as much as mine, they are nothing, or next to nothing;
LG1867	635	If they are not the riddle and the untying of the rid-dleriddle, they are nothing;
LG1867	636	If they are not just as close as they are distant, they are nothing.
LG1867	637	This is the grass that grows wherever the land is,and the water is;
LG1867	638	This is the common air that bathes the globe.
LG1867	639	With music strong I come—with my cornets and my drums,
LG1867	640	I play not marches for accepted victors only—I play great marches for conquer'd and slain persons.
LG1867	641	Have you heard that it was good to gain the day?
LG1867	642	I also say it is good to fall—battles are lost in the same spirit in which they are won.
LG1867	643	I beat and pound for the dead;
LG1867	644	I blow through my embouchures my loudest and gay-estgayest for them.
LG1867	645	Vivas to those who have fail'd!
LG1867	646	And to those whose war-vessels sank in the sea!
LG1867	647	And to those themselves who sank in the sea!
LG1867	648	And to all generals that lost engagements! and all overcome heroes!
LG1867	649	And the numberless unknown heroes, equal to the greatest heroes known.
LG1867	650	This is the meal pleasantly set—this is the meat for natural hunger;
LG1867	651	It is for the wicked just the same as the righteous—I make appointments with all;
LG1867	652	I will not have a single person slighted or left away;
LG1867	653	The kept-woman, sponger, thief, are hereby invited;
LG1867	654	The heavy-lipp'd slave is invited—the venerealee is invited:
LG1867	655	There shall be no difference between them and the rest.
LG1867	656	This is the press of a bashful hand—this is the float and odor of hair;
LG1867	657	This is the touch of my lips to yours—this is the mur-murmurmur of yearning;
LG1867	658	This is the far-off depth and height reflecting my own face;
LG1867	659	This is the thoughtful merge of myself, and the outlet < again.
LG1867	660	Do you guess I have some intricate purpose?
LG1867	661	Well, I have—for the Fourth-month showers have,and the mica on the side of a rock has.
LG1867	662	Do you take it I would astonish?
LG1867	663	Does the daylight astonish? Does the early redstart,twittering through the woods?
LG1867	664	Do I astonish more than they?
LG1867	665	This hour I tell things in confidence;
LG1867	666	I might not tell everybody, but I will tell you.
LG1867	667	Who goes there? hankering, gross, mystical, nude;
LG1867	668	How is it I extract strength from the beef I eat?
LG1867	669	What is a man, anyhow? What am I? What are you?
LG1867	670	All I mark as my own, you shall offset it with your own;
LG1867	671	Else it were time lost listening to me.
LG1867	672	I do not snivel that snivel the world over,
LG1867	673	That months are vacuums, and the ground but wal-lowwallow and filth;
LG1867	674	That life is a suck and a sell, and nothing remains at the end but threadbare crape, and tears.
LG1867	675	Whimpering and truckling fold with powders for invalids—conformity goes to the fourth-remov'd;
LG1867	676	I wear my hat as I please, indoors or out.
LG1867	677	Why should I pray? Why should I venerate and be ceremonious?
LG1867	678	Having pried through the strata, analyzed to a hair,counsell'd with doctors, and calculated close,
LG1867	679	I find no sweeter fat then sticks to my own bones.
LG1867	680	In all people I see myself—none more, and not one a barley-corn less;
LG1867	681	And the good or bad I say of myself, I say of them.
LG1867	682	And I know I am solid and sound;
LG1867	683	To me the converging objects of the universe perpetu-allyperpetually flow;
LG1867	684	All are written to me, and I must get what the writing means.
LG1867	685	I know I am deathless;
LG1867	686	I know this orbit of mine cannot be swept by the car-pentercarpenter's compass;
LG1867	687	I know I shall not pass like a child's carlacue cut with a burnt stick at night.
LG1867	688	I know I am august;
LG1867	689	I do not trouble my spirit to vindicate itself or be understood;
LG1867	690	I see that the elementary laws never apologize;
LG1867	691	(I reckon I behave no prouder than the level I plant my house by, after all.)
LG1867	692	I exist as I am—that is enough;
LG1867	693	If no other in the world be aware, I sit content;
LG1867	694	And if each and all be aware, I sit content.
LG1867	695	One world is aware, and by far the largest to me,and that is myself;
LG1867	696	And whether I come to my own to-day, or in ten thousand or ten million years,
LG1867	697	I can cheerfully take it now, or with equal cheerful-nesscheerfulness I can wait.
LG1867	698	My foothold is tenon'd and mortis'd in granite;
LG1867	699	I laugh at what you call dissolution;
LG1867	700	And I know the amplitude of time.
LG1867	701	I am the poet of the Body;
LG1867	702	And I am the poet of the Soul.
LG1867	703	The pleasures of heaven are with me, and the pains of hell are with me;
LG1867	704	The first I graft and increase upon myself—the latter I translate into a new tongue.
LG1867	705	I am the poet of the woman the same as the man;
LG1867	706	And I say it is as great to be a woman as to be a man;
LG1867	707	And I say there is nothing greater than the mother of men.
LG1867	708	I chant the chant of dilation or pride;
LG1867	709	We have had ducking and deprecating about enough;
LG1867	710	I show that size is only development.
LG1867	711	Have you outstript the rest? Are you the Presi-dentPresident?
LG1867	712	It is a trifle—they will more than arrive there, every one, and still pass on.
LG1867	713	I am he that walks with the tender and growing night;
LG1867	714	I call to the earth and sea, half-held by the night.
LG1867	715	Press close, bare-bosom'd night! Press close, mag-neticmagnetic, nourishing night!
LG1867	716	Night of south winds! night of the large few stars!
LG1867	717	Still, nodding night! mad, naked, summer night.
LG1867	718	Smile, O voluptuous, cool-breath'd earth!
LG1867	719	Earth of the slumbering and liquid trees;
LG1867	720	Earth of departed sunset! earth of the mountains,misty-topt!
LG1867	721	Earth of the vitreous pour of the full moon, just tinged with blue!
LG1867	722	Earth of shine and dark, mottling the tide of the river!
LG1867	723	Earth of the limpid gray of clouds, brighter and clearer for my sake!
LG1867	724	Far-swooping elbow'd earth! rich, apple-blossom'd earth!
LG1867	725	Smile, for your lover comes!
LG1867	726	Prodigal, you have given me love! Therefore I to you give love!
LG1867	727	O unspeakable, passionate love!
LG1867	728	You sea! I resign myself to you also—I guess what you mean;
LG1867	729	I behold from the beach your crooked inviting fingers;
LG1867	730	I believe you refuse to go back without feeling of me;
LG1867	731	We must have a turn together—I undress—hurry me out of sight of the land;
LG1867	732	Cushion me soft, rock me in billowy drowse;
LG1867	733	Sea of stretch'd ground-swells!
LG1867	734	Sea breathing broad and convulsive breaths!
LG1867	735	Sea of the brine of life! sea of unshovell'd yet always-readyalways-ready graves!
LG1867	736	Howler and scooper of storms! capricious and dainty sea!
LG1867	737	I am integral with you—I too am of one phase, and of all phases.
LG1867	738	Partaker of influx and efflux I—extoller of hate and conciliation;
LG1867	739	Extoller of amies, and those that sleep in each others' arms.
LG1867	740	I am he attesting sympathy;
LG1867	741	(Shall I make my list of things in the house, and skip the house that supports them?)
LG1867	742	I am not the poet of goodness only—I do not de-clinedecline to be the poet of wickedness also.
LG1867	743	Washes and razors for foofoos—for me freckles and a bristling beard.
LG1867	744	What blurt is this about virtue and about vice?
LG1867	745	Evil propels me, and reform of evil propels me—I stand indifferent;
LG1867	746	My gait is no fault-finder's or rejecter's gait;
LG1867	747	I moisten the roots of all that has grown.
LG1867	748	Did you fear some scrofula out of the unflagging pregnancy?
LG1867	749	Did you guess the celestial laws are yet to be work'd over and rectified?
LG1867	750	I find one side a balance, and the antipodal side a balance;
LG1867	751	Soft doctrine as steady help as stable doctrine;
LG1867	752	Thoughts and deeds of the present, our rouse and early start.
LG1867	753	This minute that comes to me over the past decil-lionsdecillions,
LG1867	754	There is no better than it and now.
LG1867	755	What behaved well in the past, or behaves well to-day, is not such a wonder;
LG1867	756	The wonder is, always and always, how there can be a mean man or an infidel.
LG1867	757	Endless unfolding of words of ages!
LG1867	758	And mine a word of the modern—the word En-masseEn-masse.
LG1867	759	A word of the faith that never balks;
LG1867	760	Here or henceforward, it is all the same to me— I accept time, absolutely.
LG1867	761	It alone is without flaw—it rounds and completes all;
LG1867	762	That mystic, baffling wonder I love, alone completes all.
LG1867	763	I accept reality, and dare not question it;
LG1867	764	Materialism first and last imbuing.
LG1867	765	Hurrah for positive science! long live exact demon-strationdemonstration!
LG1867	766	Fetch stonecrop, mixt with cedar and branches of lilac;
LG1867	767	This is the lexicographer—this the chemist—this made a grammar of the old cartouches;
LG1867	768	These mariners put the ship through dangerous un-knownunknown seas;
LG1867	769	This is the geologist—this works with the scalpel— and this is a mathematician.
LG1867	770	Gentlemen! to you the first honors always:
LG1867	771	Your facts are useful and real—and yet they are not my dwelling;
LG1867	772	(I but enter by them to an area of my dwelling.)
LG1867	773	Less the reminders of properties told, my words;
LG1867	774	And more the reminders, they, of life untold, and of freedom and extrication,
LG1867	775	And make short account of neuters and geldings, and favor men and women fully equipt,
LG1867	776	And beat the gong of revolt, and stop with fugitives,and them that plot and conspire.
LG1867	777	Walt Whitman am I, of mighty Manhattan the son,
LG1867	778	Turbulent, fleshy and sensual, eating, drinking and breeding;
LG1867	779	No sentimentalist—no stander above men and women,or apart from them;
LG1867	780	No more modest than immodest.
LG1867	781	Unscrew the locks from the doors!
LG1867	782	Unscrew the doors themselves from their jambs!
LG1867	783	Whoever degrades another degrades me;
LG1867	784	And whatever is done or said returns at last to me.
LG1867	785	Through me the afflatus surging and surging— through me the current and index.
LG1867	786	I speak the pass-word primeval—I give the sign of democracy;
LG1867	787	By God! I will accept nothing which all cannot have their counterpart of on the same terms.
LG1867	788	Through me many long dumb voices;
LG1867	789	Voices of the interminable generations of slaves;
LG1867	790	Voices of prostitutes, and of deform'd persons;
LG1867	791	Voices of the diseas'd and despairing, and of thieves and dwarfs;
LG1867	792	Voices of cycles of preparation and accretion,
LG1867	793	And of the threads that connect the stars—and of wombs, and of the fatherstuff,
LG1867	794	And of the rights of them the others are down upon;
LG1867	795	Of the trivial, flat, foolish, despised,
LG1867	796	Fog in the air, beetles rolling balls of dung.
LG1867	797	Through me forbidden voices;
LG1867	798	Voices of sexes and lusts—voices veil'd, and I remove the veil;
LG1867	799	Voices indecent, by me clarified and transfigur'd.
LG1867	800	I do not press my fingers across my mouth;
LG1867	801	I keep as delicate around the bowels as around the head and heart;
LG1867	802	Copulation is no more rank to me than death is.
LG1867	803	I believe in the flesh and the appetites;
LG1867	804	Seeing, hearing, feeling, are miracles, and each part and tag of me is a miracle.
LG1867	805	Divine am I inside and out, and I make holy what-everwhatever I touch or am touch'd from;
LG1867	806	The scent of these arm-pits, aroma finer than prayer;
LG1867	807	This head more than churches, bibles, and all the creeds.
LG1867	808	If I worship one thing more than another, it shall be the spread of my own body, or any part of it.
LG1867	809	Translucent mould of me, it shall be you!
LG1867	810	Shaded ledges and rests, it shall be you!
LG1867	811	Firm masculine colter, it shall be you.
LG1867	812	Whatever goes to the tilth of me, it shall be you!
LG1867	813	You my rich blood! Your milky stream, pale strip-pingsstrippings of my life.
LG1867	814	Breast that presses against other breasts, it shall be you!
LG1867	815	My brain, it shall be your occult convolutions.
LG1867	816	Root of wash't sweet-flag! timorous pond-snipe!nest of guarded duplicate eggs! it shall be you!
LG1867	817	Mix't tussled hay of head, beard, brawn, it shall be you!
LG1867	818	Trickling sap of maple! fibre of manly wheat! it shall be you!
LG1867	819	Sun so generous, it shall be you!
LG1867	820	Vapors lighting and shading my face, it shall be you!
LG1867	821	You sweaty brooks and dews, it shall be you!
LG1867	822	Winds whose soft-tickling genitals rub against me, it shall be you!

LG1867	824	Hands I have taken—face I have kiss'd—mortal I have ever touch'd! it shall be you.
LG1867	825	I dote on myself—there is that lot of me, and all so luscious;
LG1867	826	Each moment, and whatever happens, thrills me with joy.
LG1867	827	O I am wonderful!
LG1867	828	I cannot tell how my ankles bend, nor whence the cause of my faintest wish;
LG1867	829	Nor the cause of the friendship I emit, nor the cause of the friendship I take again.
LG1867	830	That I walk up my stoop! I pause to consider if it really be;
LG1867	831	A morning-glory at my window satisfies me more than the metaphysics of books.
LG1867	832	To behold the day-break!
LG1867	833	The little light fades the immense and diaphanous shadows;
LG1867	834	The air tastes good to my palate.
LG1867	835	Hefts of the moving world, at innocent gambols silently rising, freshly exuding,
LG1867	836	Scooting obliquely high and low.
LG1867	837	Something I cannot see puts upward libidinous prongs;
LG1867	838	Seas of bright juice suffuse heaven.
LG1867	839	The earth by the sky staid with—the daily close of their junction;
LG1867	840	The heav'd challenge from the east that moment over my head;
LG1867	841	The mocking taunt, See then whether you shall be master!
LG1867	842	Dazzling and tremendous, how quick the sun-rise would kill me,
LG1867	843	If I could not now and always send sun-rise out of me.
LG1867	844	We also ascend, dazzling and tremendous as the sun;
LG1867	845	We found our own, O my Soul, in the calm and cool of the day-break.
LG1867	846	My voice goes after what my eyes cannot reach;
LG1867	847	With the twirl of my tongue I encompass worlds, and volumes of worlds.
LG1867	848	Speech is the twin of my vision—it is unequal to measure itself;
LG1867	849	It provokes me forever;
LG1867	850	It says sarcastically, Walt, you contain enough—why don't you let it out then?
LG1867	851	Come now, I will not be tantalized—you conceive too much of articulation.
LG1867	852	Do you not know, O speech, how the buds beneath you are folded?
LG1867	853	Waiting in gloom, protected by frost;
LG1867	854	The dirt receding before my prophetical screams;
LG1867	855	I underlying causes, to balance them at last;
LG1867	856	My knowledge my live parts—it keeping tally with the meaning of things;
LG1867	857	Happiness—which, whoever hears me, let him or her set out in search of this day.
LG1867	858	My final merit I refuse you—I refuse putting from me what I really am;
LG1867	859	Encompass worlds, but never try to encompass me;
LG1867	860	I crowd your sleekest and best by simply looking to-wardtoward you.
LG1867	861	Writing and talk do not prove me;
LG1867	862	I carry the plenum of proof, and everything else, in my face;
LG1867	863	With the hush of my lips I wholly confound the skep-ticskeptic.
LG1867	864	I think I will do nothing now but listen,
LG1867	865	To accrue what I hear into myself—to let sounds con-tributecontribute toward me.
LG1867	866	I hear bravuras of birds, bustle of growing wheat,gossip of flames, clack of sticks cooking my meals;
LG1867	867	I hear the sound I love, the sound of the human voice;
LG1867	868	I hear all sounds running together, combined, fused or following;
LG1867	869	Sounds of the city, and sounds out of the city—sounds of the day and night;
LG1867	870	Talkative young ones to those that like them—the loud laugh of work-people at their meals;
LG1867	871	The angry base of disjointed friendship—the faint tones of the sick;
LG1867	872	The judge with hands tight to the desk, his pallid lips pronouncing a death-sentence;
LG1867	873	The heave'e'yo of stevedores unlading ships by the wharves—the refrain of the anchor-lifters;

LG1867	875	The steam-whistle—the solid roll of the train of ap-proachingapproaching cars;
LG1867	876	The slow-march play'd at the head of the association,marching two and two;
LG1867	877	(They go to guard some corpse—the flag-tops are draped with black muslin.)
LG1867	878	I hear the violoncello, ('tis the young man's heart's complaint;)
LG1867	879	I hear the key'd cornet—it glides quickly in through my ears;
LG1867	880	It shakes mad-sweet pangs through my belly and breast.
LG1867	881	I hear the chorus—it is a grand opera;
LG1867	882	Ah, this indeed is music! This suits me.
LG1867	883	A tenor large and fresh as the creation fills me;
LG1867	884	The orbic flex of his mouth is pouring and filling me full.
LG1867	885	I hear the train'd soprano—(what work, with hers,is this?)
LG1867	886	The orchestra wrenches such ardors from me, I did not know I possess'd them;
LG1867	887	It sails me—I dab with bare feet—they are lick'd by the indolent waves;
LG1867	888	I am exposed, cut by bitter and angry hail—I lose my breath,
LG1867	889	Steep'd amid honey'd morphine, my windpipe throt-tledthrottled in fakes of death;
LG1867	890	At length let up again to feel the puzzle of puzzles,
LG1867	891	And that we call BEING.
LG1867	892	To be, in any form—what is that?
LG1867	893	(Round and round we go, all of us, and ever come back thither;)
LG1867	894	If nothing lay more develop't, the quahaug in its cal-louscallous shell were enough.
LG1867	895	Mine is no callous shell;
LG1867	896	I have instant conductors all over me, whether I pass or stop;
LG1867	897	They seize every object, and lead it harmlessly through me.
LG1867	898	I merely stir, press, feel with my fingers, and am happy;
LG1867	899	To touch my person to some one else's is about as much as I can stand.
LG1867	900	Is this then a touch? quivering me to a new identity,
LG1867	901	Flames and ether making a rush for my veins,
LG1867	902	Treacherous tip of me reaching and crowding to help them,
LG1867	903	My flesh and blood playing out lightning to strike what is hardly different from myself;
LG1867	904	On all sides prurient provokers stiffening my limbs,
LG1867	905	Straining the udder of my heart for its withheld drip,
LG1867	906	Behaving licentious toward me, taking no denial,
LG1867	907	Depriving me of my best, as for a purpose,
LG1867	908	Unbuttoning my clothes, holding me by the bare waist,
LG1867	909	Deluding my confusion with the calm of the sunlight and pasture-fields,
LG1867	910	Immodestly sliding the fellow-senses away,
LG1867	911	They bribed to swap off with touch, and go and graze at the edges of me;
LG1867	912	No consideration, no regard for my draining strength or my anger;
LG1867	913	Fetching the rest of the herd around to enjoy them a while,
LG1867	914	Then all uniting to stand on a headland and worry me.
LG1867	915	The sentries desert every other part of me;
LG1867	916	They have left me helpless to a red marauder;
LG1867	917	They all come to the headland, to witness and assist against me.
LG1867	918	I am given up by traitors;
LG1867	919	I talk wildly—I have lost my wits—I and nobody else am the greatest traitor;
LG1867	920	I went myself first to the headland—my own hands carried me there.
LG1867	921	You villain touch! what are you doing? My breath is tight in its throat;
LG1867	922	Unclench your floodgates! you are too much for me.
LG1867	923	Blind, loving, wrestling touch! sheath'd, hooded,sharp-tooth'd touch!
LG1867	924	Did it make you ache so, leaving me?
LG1867	925	Parting, track't by arriving—perpetual payment of perpetual loan;
LG1867	926	Rich, showering rain, and recompense richer after-wardafterward.
LG1867	927	Sprouts take and accumulate—stand by the curb prolific and vital;
LG1867	928	Landscapes, projected, masculine, full-sized, and golden.
LG1867	929	All truths wait in all things;
LG1867	930	They neither hasten their own delivery, nor resist it;
LG1867	931	They do not need the obstetric forceps of the surgeon;
LG1867	932	The insignificant is as big to me as any;
LG1867	933	(What is less or more than a touch?)
LG1867	934	Logic and sermons never convince;
LG1867	935	The damp of the night drives deeper into my soul.
LG1867	936	Only what proves itself to every man and woman is so;
LG1867	937	Only what nobody denies is so.
LG1867	938	A minute and a drop of me settle my brain;
LG1867	939	I believe the soggy clods shall become lovers and lamps,
LG1867	940	And a compend of compends is the meat of a man or woman,
LG1867	941	And a summit and flower there is the feeling they have for each other,
LG1867	942	And they are to branch boundlessly out of that lesson,until it becomes omnific,
LG1867	943	And until every one shall delight us, and we them.
LG1867	944	I believe a leaf of grass is no less than the journey-workjourney-work of the stars,
LG1867	945	And the pismire is equally perfect, and a grain of sand, and the egg of the wren,
LG1867	946	And the tree-toad is a chef-d'oeuvre for the highest,
LG1867	947	And the running blackberry would adorn the parlors of heaven,
LG1867	948	And the narrowest hinge in my hand puts to scorn all machinery,
LG1867	949	And the cow crunching with depres't head surpasses any statue,
LG1867	950	And a mouse is miracle enough to stagger sextillions of infidels,
LG1867	951	And I could come every afternoon of my life to look at the farmer's girl boiling her iron ten-kettle and baking short-cake.
LG1867	952	I find I incorporate gneiss, coal, long-threaded moss,fruits, grains, esculent roots,
LG1867	953	And am stuccoed with quadrupeds and birds all over,
LG1867	954	And have distanced what is behind me for good reasons,
LG1867	955	And call anything close again, when I desire it.
LG1867	956	In vain the speeding or shyness;
LG1867	957	In vain the plutonic rocks send their old heat against my approach;
LG1867	958	In vain the mastadon retreats beneath its own pow-derpowder'd bones;
LG1867	959	In vain objects stand leagues off, and assume manifold shapes;
LG1867	960	In vain the ocean settling in hollows, and the great monsters lying low;
LG1867	961	In vain the buzzard houses herself with the sky;
LG1867	962	In vain the snake slides through the creepers and logs;
LG1867	963	In vain the elk takes to the inner passes of the woods;
LG1867	964	In vain the razor-bill'd auk sails far north to Labrador;
LG1867	965	I follow quickly, I ascend to the nest in the fissure of the cliff.
LG1867	966	I think I could turn and live with animals, they are so placid and self-contain'd;
LG1867	967	I stand and look at them long and long.
LG1867	968	They do not sweat and whine about their condition;
LG1867	969	They do not lie awake in the dark and weep for their sins;
LG1867	970	They do not make me sick discussing their duty to God;
LG1867	971	Not one is dissatisfied—not one is demented with the mania of owning things;
LG1867	972	Not one kneels to another, nor to his kind that lived thousands of years ago;
LG1867	973	Not one is respectable or industrious over the whole earth.
LG1867	974	So they show their relations to me, and I accept them;
LG1867	975	They bring me tokens of myself—they evince them plainly in their possession.
LG1867	976	I wonder where they get those tokens:
LG1867	977	Did I pass that way huge times ago, and negligently drop them?
LG1867	978	Myself moving forward then and now and forever,
LG1867	979	Gathering and showing more always and with velocity,
LG1867	980	Infinite and omnigenous, and the like of these among them;
LG1867	981	Not too exclusive toward the reachers of my remem-brancersremembrancers;
LG1867	982	Picking out here one that I love, and now go with him on brotherly terms.
LG1867	983	A gigantic beauty of a stallion, fresh and responsive to my caresses,
LG1867	984	Head high in the forehead, wide between the ears,
LG1867	985	Limbs glossy and supple, tail dusting the ground,
LG1867	986	Eyes well apart, full of sparkling wickedness—ears finely cut, flexibly moving.
LG1867	987	His nostrils dilate, as my heels embrace him;
LG1867	988	His well-built limbs tremble with pleasure, as we speed around and return.
LG1867	989	I but use you a moment, then I resign you, stallion;
LG1867	990	Why do I need your paces, when I myself out-gallop them?
LG1867	991	Even, as I stand or sit, passing faster than you.
LG1867	992	O swift wind! O space and time! now I see it is true, what I guess'd at;
LG1867	993	What I guess'd when I loaf'd on the grass;
LG1867	994	What I guess'd while I lay alone in my bed,

LG1867	1154	Now I tell what I knew in Texas in my early youth;
LG1867	1155	(I tell not the fall of Alamo,
LG1867	1156	Not one escaped to tell the fall of Alamo,
LG1867	1157	The hundred and fifty are dumb yet at Alamo;)
LG1867	1158	Tis the tale of the murder in cold blood of four hun-dredhundred and twelve young men.
LG1867	1159	Retreating, they had form'd in a hollow square, with their baggage for breastworks;
LG1867	1160	Nine hundred lives out of the surrounding enemy's,nine times their number, was the price they took in advance;
LG1867	1161	Their colonel was wounded and their ammunition gone;
LG1867	1162	They treated for an honorable capitulation, receiv'd writing and seal, gave up their arms, and march'd back prisoners of war.
LG1867	1163	They were the glory of the race of rangers;
LG1867	1164	Matchless with horse, rifle, song, supper, courtship,
LG1867	1165	Large, turbulent, generous, brave, handsome, proud,and affectionate,
LG1867	1166	Bearded, sunburnt, drest in the free costume of hunters,
LG1867	1167	Not a single one over thirty years of age.
LG1867	1168	The second First-day morning they were brought out in squads, and massacred—it was beautiful early summer;
LG1867	1169	The work commenced about five o'clock, and was over by eight.
LG1867	1170	None obey'd the command to kneel;
LG1867	1171	Some made a mad and helpless rush—some stood stark and straight;

LG1867	1176	A youth not seventeen years old seiz'd his assassin till two more came to release him;
LG1867	1177	The three were all torn, and cover'd with the boy's blood.

LG1867	1179	That is the tale of the murder of the four hundred and twelve young men.
LG1867	1180	Would you hear of an old-fashion'd sea-fight?
LG1867	1181	Would you learn who won by the light of the moon and stars?
LG1867	1182	List to the story as my grandmother's father, the sailor, told it to me.
LG1867	1183	Our foe was no skulk in his ship, I tell you, (said he;)
LG1867	1184	His was the surly English pluck—and there is no tougher or truer, and never was, and never will be;
LG1867	1185	Along the lower'd eve he came, horribly raking us.
LG1867	1186	We closed with him—the yards entangled—the cannon touch'd;
LG1867	1187	My captain lash'd fast with his own hands.
LG1867	1188	We had receiv'd some eighteen-pound shots under the water;
LG1867	1189	On our lower-gun-deck two large pieces had burst at the first fire, killing all around, and blowing up overhead.
LG1867	1190	Fighting at sun-down, fighting at dark;
LG1867	1191	Ten o'clock at night, the full moon well up, our leaks on the gain, and five feet of water reported;
LG1867	1192	The master-at-arms loosing the prisoners confined in the after-hold, to give them a chance for them-selvesthemselves.
LG1867	1193	The transit to and from the magazine is now stopt by the sentinels,
LG1867	1194	They see so many strange faces, they do not know whom to trust.
LG1867	1195	Our frigate takes fire;
LG1867	1196	The other asks if we demand quarter?
LG1867	1197	If our colors are struck, and the fighting is done?
LG1867	1198	Now I laugh content, for I hear the voice of my little captain,(says my grandmother's father;)
LG1867	1199	We have not struck, he composedly cries, we have just begun our part of the fighting.
LG1867	1200	Only three guns are in use;
LG1867	1201	One is directed by the captain himself against the enemy's main-mast;
LG1867	1202	Two, well served with grape and canister, silence his musketry and clear his decks.
LG1867	1203	The tops alone second the fire of this little battery,especially the main-top;
LG1867	1204	They hold out bravely during the whole of the action.
LG1867	1205	Not a moment's cease;
LG1867	1206	The leaks gain fast on the pumps—the fire eats toward the powder-magazine;
LG1867	1207	One of the pumps has been shot away—it is generally thought we are sinking.
LG1867	1208	Serene stands the little captain;
LG1867	1209	He is not hurried—his voice is neither high nor low;
LG1867	1210	His eyes give more light to us than our battle-lanternsbattle-lanterns.
LG1867	1211	Toward twelve at night, there in the beams of the moon, they surrender to us.
LG1867	1212	O now it is not my grandmother's father there in the fight;
LG1867	1213	I feel it is I myself.
LG1867	1214	Stretch'd and still lies the midnight;
LG1867	1215	Two great hulls motionless on the breast of the darkness;
LG1867	1216	Our vessel riddled and slowly sinking—preparations to pass to the one we have conquer'd;

LG1867	1218	Near by, the corpse of the child that serv'd in the cabin;

LG1867	1227	O Christ! This is mastering me!
LG1867	1228	Through the conquer'd doors they crowd. I am possess'd.
LG1867	1229	I embody all presences outlaw'd or suffering;
LG1867	1230	See myself in prison shaped like another man,

LG1867	1232	For me the keepers of convicts shoulder their car-binescarbines and keep watch;
