LG1867	5231	I withdraw from the still woods I loved;
LG1867	5232	I will not go now on the pastures to walk;
LG1867	5233	I will not strip the clothes from my body to meet my lover the sea;
LG1867	5234	I will not touch my flesh to the earth, as to other flesh, to renew me.
LG1867	5235	O how can the ground not sicken?
LG1867	5236	How can you be alive, you growths of spring?
LG1867	5237	How can you furnish health, you blood of herbs, roots, orchards, grain?
LG1867	5238	Are they not continually putting distemper'd corpses in you?
LG1867	5239	Is not every continent work'd over and over with sour dead?
LG1867	5240	Where have you disposed of their carcasses?
LG1867	5241	Those drunkards and gluttons of so many genera-tionsgenerations;
LG1867	5242	Where have you drawn off all the foul liquid and meat?
LG1867	5243	I do not see any of it upon you to-day—or perhaps I am deceiv'd;
LG1867	5244	I will run a furrow with my plough—I will press my spade through the sod, and turn it up under-neathunderneath;
LG1867	5245	I am sure I shall expose some of the foul meat.
LG1867	5246	Behold this compost! behold it well!
LG1867	5247	Perhaps every mite has once form'd part of a sick person—Yet behold!
LG1867	5248	The grass covers the prairies,
LG1867	5249	The bean bursts noiselessly through the mould in the garden,
LG1867	5250	The delicate spear of the onion pierces upward,
LG1867	5251	The apple-buds cluster together on the apple-branches,
LG1867	5252	The resurrection of the wheat appears with pale visage out of its graves,
LG1867	5253	The tinge awakes over the willow-tree and the mul-berrymulberry-tree-tree,
LG1867	5254	The he-birds carol mornings and evenings, while the she-birds sit on their nests,
LG1867	5255	The young of poultry break through the hatch'd eggs,
LG1867	5256	The new-born of animals appear—the calf is dropt from the cow, the colt from the mare,
LG1867	5257	Out of its little hill faithfully rise the potato's dark green leaves,
LG1867	5258	Out of its hill rises the yellow maize-stalk;
LG1867	5259	The summer growth is innocent and disdainful above all those strata of sour dead.
LG1867	5260	What chemistry!
LG1867	5261	That the winds are really not infectious,
LG1867	5262	That this is no cheat, this transparent green-wash of the sea, which is so amorous after me,
LG1867	5263	That it is safe to allow it to lick my naked body all over with its tongues,
LG1867	5264	That it will not endanger me with the fevers that have deposited themselves in it,
LG1867	5265	That all is clean forever and forever,
LG1867	5266	That the cool drink from the well tastes so good,
LG1867	5267	That blackberries are so flavorous and juicy,
LG1867	5268	That the fruits of the apple-orchard, and of the orange-orchard—that melons, grapes, peaches, plums, will none of them poison me,
LG1867	5269	That when I recline on the grass I do not catch any disease,
LG1867	5270	Though probably every spear of grass rises out of what was once a catching disease.
LG1867	5271	Now I am terrified at the earth! it is that calm and patient,
LG1867	5272	It grows such sweet things out of such corruptions,
LG1867	5273	It turns harmless and stainless on its axis, with such endless successions of diseas'd corpses,
LG1867	5274	It distils such exquisite winds out of such infused fetor,
LG1867	5275	It renews with such unwitting looks, its prodigal, annual, sumptuous crops,
LG1867	5276	It gives such divine materials to men, and accepts such leavings from them at last.
LG1867	5277	I HEAR America singing, the varied carols I hear;
LG1867	5278	Those of mechanics—each one singing his, as it should be, blithe and strong;
LG1867	5279	The carpenter singing his, as he measures his plank or beam,
LG1867	5280	The mason singing his, as he makes ready for work, or leaves off work;
LG1867	5281	The boatman singing what belongs to him in his boat —the deck-hand singing on the steamboat deck;
LG1867	5282	The shoemaker singing as he sits on his bench—the hatter singing as he stands;
LG1867	5283	The wood-cutter's song—the ploughboy's, on his way in the morning, or at the noon intermission, or at sundown;
LG1867	5284	The delicious singing of the mother—or of the young wife at work—or of the girl sewing or washing —Each singing what belongs to her, and to none else;
LG1867	5285	The day what belongs to the day—At night, the party of young fellows, robust, friendly,
LG1867	5286	Singing, with open mouths, their strong melodious songs.
LG1867	5287	MANHATTAN'S streets I saunter'd pondering,
LG1867	5288	On time, space, reality—on such as these, and abreast with them, prudence.
LG1867	5289	After all, the last explanation remains to be made about prudence,
LG1867	5290	Little and large alike drop quietly aside from the pru-denceprudence that suits immortality.
LG1867	5291	The Soul is of itself;
LG1867	5292	All verges to it—all has reference to what ensues;
LG1867	5293	All that a person does, says, thinks, is of consequence;
LG1867	5294	Not a move can a man or woman make, that affects him or her in a day, month, any part of the di-rectdirect life-time, or the hour of death, but the same affects him or her onward afterward through the indirect life-time.
LG1867	5295	The indirect is just as much as the direct,
LG1867	5296	The spirit receives from the body just as much as it gives to the body, if not more.
LG1867	5297	Not one word or deed—not venereal sore, discolor-ationdiscoloration, privacy of the onanist, putridity of glut-tonsgluttons or rum-drinkers, peculation, cunning, betrayal, murder, seduction, prostitution, but has results beyond death, as really as before death.
LG1867	5298	Charity and personal force are the only investments worth anything.
LG1867	5299	No specification is necessary—all that a male or fe-malefemale does, that is vigorous, benevolent, clean, is so much profit to him or her, in the unshak-ableunshakable order of the universe, and through the whole scope of it forever.
LG1867	5300	Who has been wise, receives interest,
LG1867	5301	Savage, felon, President, judge, farmer, sailor, me-chanicmechanic, literat, young, old, it is the same,
LG1867	5302	The interest will come round—all will come round.
LG1867	5303	Singly, wholly, to affect now, affected their time, will forever affect, all of the past, and all of the present, and all of the future,
LG1867	5304	All the brave actions of war and peace,
LG1867	5305	All help given to relatives, strangers, the poor, old, sorrowful, young children, widows, the sick, and to shunned persons,
LG1867	5306	All furtherance of fugitives, and of the escape of slaves,
LG1867	5307	All self-denial that stood steady and aloof on wrecks, and saw others fill the seats of the boats,
LG1867	5308	All offering of substance or life for the good old cause, or for a friend's sake, or opinion's sake,
LG1867	5309	All pains of enthusiasts, scoffed at by their neighbors,
LG1867	5310	All the limitless sweet love and precious suffering of mothers,
LG1867	5311	All honest men baffled in strifes recorded or unre-cordedunrecorded,
LG1867	5312	All the grandeur and good of ancient nations whose fragments we inherit,
LG1867	5313	All the good of the hundreds of ancient nations un-knownunknown to us by name, date, location,

LG1867	5315	All suggestions of the divine mind of man, or the divinity of his mouth, or the shaping of his great hands;
LG1867	5316	All that is well thought or said this day on any part of the globe—or on any of the wandering stars, or on any of the fix'd stars, by those there as we are here,
LG1867	5317	All that is henceforth to be thought or done by you, whoever you are, or by any one,
LG1867	5318	These inure, have inured, shall inure, to the identities from which they sprang, or shall spring.
LG1867	5319	Did you guess anything lived only its moment?
LG1867	5320	The world does not so exist—no parts palpable or impalpable so exist,
LG1867	5321	No consummation exists without being from some long previous consummation—and that from some other,
LG1867	5322	Without the farthest conceivable one coming a bit nearer the beginning than any.
LG1867	5323	Whatever satisfies Souls is true;
LG1867	5324	Prudence entirely satisfies the craving and glut of Souls;
LG1867	5325	Itself only finally satisfies the Soul;
LG1867	5326	The Soul has that measureless pride which revolts from every lesson but its own.
LG1867	5327	Now I give you an inkling;
LG1867	5328	Now I breathe the word of the prudence that walks abreast with time, space, reality,
LG1867	5329	That answers the pride which refuses every lesson but its own.
LG1867	5330	What is prudence, is indivisible,
LG1867	5331	Declines to separate one part of life from every part,
LG1867	5332	Divides not the righteous from the unrighteous, or the living from the dead,
LG1867	5333	Matches every thought or act by its correlative,
LG1867	5334	Knows no possible forgiveness or deputed atonement,
LG1867	5335	Knows that the young man who composedly peril'd his life and lost it, has done exceedingly well for himself, without doubt,
LG1867	5336	That he who never peril'd his life, but retains it to old age in riches and ease, has probably achiev'd nothing for himself worth mentioning;
LG1867	5337	Knows that only that person has really learn'd who has learn'd to prefer results,
LG1867	5338	Who favors Body and Soul the same,
LG1867	5339	Who perceives the indirect assuredly following the direct,
LG1867	5340	Who in his spirit in any emergency whatever neither hurries or avoids death.
LG1867	5341	I WAS looking a long while for the history of the past for myself, and for these chants—and now I have found it;
LG1867	5342	It is not in those paged fables in the libraries, (them I neither accept nor reject;)
LG1867	5343	It is no more in the legends than in all else;
LG1867	5344	It is in the present—it is this earth to-day;
LG1867	5345	It is in Democracy—in this America—the old world also;
LG1867	5346	It is the life of one man or one woman to-day, the average man of to-day;
LG1867	5347	It is languages, social customs, literatures, arts;
LG1867	5348	It is the broad show of artificial things, ships, ma-chinerymachinery, politics, creeds, modern improvements, and the interchange of nations,

LG1867	5350	THE indications, and tally of time;
LG1867	5351	Perfect sanity shows the master among philosophs;
LG1867	5352	Time, always without flaw, indicates itself in parts;
LG1867	5353	What always indicates the poet, is the crowd of the pleasant company of singers, and their words;
LG1867	5354	The words of the singers are the hours or minutes of the light or dark—but the words of the maker of poems are the general light and dark;
LG1867	5355	The maker of poems settles justice, reality, immor-talityimmortality,
LG1867	5356	His insight and power encircle things and the human race,
LG1867	5357	He is the glory and extract, thus far, of things, and of the human race.
LG1867	5358	The singers do not beget—only the POET begets;
LG1867	5359	The singers are welcom'd, understood, appear often enough—but rare has the day been, likewise the spot, of the birth of the maker of poems,
LG1867	5360	Not every century, or every five centuries, has con-taincontain'd such a day, for all its names.
LG1867	5361	The singers of successive hours of centuries may have ostensible names, but the name of each of them is one of the singers,
LG1867	5362	The name of each is, eye-singer, ear-singer, head-singerhead-singer, sweet-singer, echo-singer, parlor-singer, love-singer, or something else.

LG1867	5364	The greatness of sons is the exuding of the greatness of mothers and fathers,
LG1867	5365	The words of poems are the tuft and final applause of science.
LG1867	5366	Divine instinct, breadth of vision, the law of rea-sonreason, health, rudeness of body, withdrawnness, gayety, sun-tan, air-sweetness—such are some of the words of poems.
LG1867	5367	The sailor and traveler underlie the maker of poems,
LG1867	5368	The builder, geometer, chemist, anatomist, phrenolo-gistphrenologist, artist—all these underlie the maker of poems.
LG1867	5369	The words of the true poems give you more than poems,
LG1867	5370	They give you to form for yourself, poems, religions, politics, war, peace, behavior, histories, essays, romances, and everything else,
LG1867	5371	They balance ranks, colors, races, creeds, and the sexes,
LG1867	5372	They do not seek beauty—they are sought,
LG1867	5373	Forever touching them, or close upon them, follows beauty, longing, fain, love-sick.
LG1867	5374	They prepare for death—yet are they not the finish, but rather the outset,
LG1867	5375	They bring none to his or her terminus, or to be con-tentcontent and full;
LG1867	5376	Whom they take, they take into space, to behold the birth of stars, to behold one of the meanings,
LG1867	5377	To launch off with absolute faith—to sweep through the ceaseless rings, and never be quiet again.
LG1867	5378	ON the beach at night alone,
LG1867	5379	As the old mother sways her to and fro, singing her savage and husky song,

LG1867	5381	A VAST SIMILITUDE interlocks all,
LG1867	5382	All spheres, grown, ungrown, small, large, suns, moons,planets, comets, asteroids,

LG1867	5393	TO ORATISTS—to male or female,
LG1867	5394	Vocalism, breath, measure, concentration, determina-tiondetermination, and the divine power to use words.
LG1867	5395	Are you full-lung'd and limber-lipp'd from long trial? from vigorous practice? from physique?
LG1867	5396	Do you move in these broad lands as broad as they?
LG1867	5397	Come duly to the divine power to use words?
LG1867	5398	For only at last, after many years—after chastity,friendship, procreation, prudence, and naked-nessnakedness;

LG1867	5400	After a loosen'd throat—after absorbing eras, temper-amentstemperaments, races—after knowledge, freedom,crimes;

LG1867	5409	Of a great vocalism, the merciless light thereof shall pour, and the storm rage,
LG1867	5410	Every flash shall be a revelation, an insult,
LG1867	5411	The glaring flame on depths, on heights, on suns, on stars,
LG1867	5412	On the interior and exterior of man or woman,
LG1867	5413	On the laws of Nature—on passive materials,

LG1867	5416	LAWS for Creations,
LG1867	5417	For strong artists and leaders—for fresh broods of teachers, and perfect literats for America,
LG1867	5418	For diverse savans, and coming musicians.
LG1867	5419	All must have reference to the ensemble of the world, and the compact truth of the world;

LG1867	5421	What do you suppose creation is?
LG1867	5422	What do you suppose will satisfy the Soul, except to walk free, and own no superior?
LG1867	5423	What do you suppose I have intimated to you in a hundred ways, but that man or woman is as good as God?
LG1867	5424	And that there is no God any more divine than Your-selfYourself?

LG1867	5427	POETS to come!
LG1867	5428	Not to-day is to justify me, and Democracy, and what we are for;
LG1867	5429	But you, a new brood, native, athletic, continental,greater than before known,

LG1867	5431	I but write one or two indicative words for the future,
LG1867	5432	I but advance a moment, only to wheel and hurry back in the darkness.
LG1867	5433	I am a man who, sauntering along, without fully stopping, turns a casual look upon you, and then averts his face,
LG1867	5434	Leaving it to you to prove and define it,
LG1867	5435	Expecting the main things from you.
LG1867	5436	ME imperturbe, standing at ease in Nature,
LG1867	5437	Master of all, or mistress of all—aplomb in the midst of irrational things,
LG1867	5438	Imbued as they—passive, receptive, silent as they,
LG1867	5439	Finding my occupation, poverty, notoriety, foibles, crimes, less important than I thought;
LG1867	5440	Me private, or public, or menial, or solitary—all these subordinate, (I am eternally equal with the best—I am not subordinate;)
LG1867	5441	Me toward the Mexican Sea, or in the Mannahatta, or the Tennessee, or far north, or inland,
LG1867	5442	A river-man, or a man of the woods, or of any farm-lifefarm-life of These States, or of the coast, or the lakes, or Kanada,
LG1867	5443	Me, wherever my life is lived, O to be self-balanced for contingencies!
LG1867	5444	O to confront night, storms, hunger, ridicule, acci-dentsaccidents, rebuffs, as the trees and animals do.
LG1867	5445	I WANDER all night in my vision,
LG1867	5446	Stepping with light feet, swiftly and noiselessly step-pingstepping and stopping,
LG1867	5447	Bending with open eyes over the shut eyes of sleepers,
LG1867	5448	Wandering and confused, lost to myself, ill-assorted,contradictory,
LG1867	5449	Pausing, gazing, bending, and stopping.
LG1867	5450	How solemn they look there, stretch'd and still!
LG1867	5451	How quiet they breathe, the little children in their cradles!
LG1867	5452	The wretched features of ennuyés, the white fea-turesfeatures of corpses, the livid faces of drunkards,the sick-gray faces of onanists,
LG1867	5453	The gash'd bodies on battle-fields, the insane in their strong-door'd rooms, the sacred idiots, the new-bornnew-born emerging from gates, and the dying emer-gingemerging from gates,
LG1867	5454	The night pervades them and infolds them.
LG1867	5455	The married couple sleep calmly in their bed—he with his palm on the hip of the wife, and she with her palm on the hip of the husband,
LG1867	5456	The sisters sleep lovingly side by side in their bed,
LG1867	5457	The men sleep lovingly side by side in theirs,
LG1867	5458	And the mother sleeps, with her little child carefully wrap't.
LG1867	5459	The blind sleep, and the deaf and dumb sleep,
LG1867	5460	The prisoner sleeps well in the prison—the run-away son sleeps;
LG1867	5461	The murderer that is to be hung next day—how does he sleep?
LG1867	5462	And the murder'd person—how does he sleep?
LG1867	5463	The female that loves unrequited sleeps,
LG1867	5464	And the male that loves unrequited sleeps,
LG1867	5465	The head of the money-maker that plotted all day sleeps,
LG1867	5466	And the enraged and treacherous dispositions—all,all sleep.
LG1867	5467	I stand in the dark with drooping eyes by the worst-suffering and the most restless,
LG1867	5468	I pass my hands soothingly to and fro a few inches from them,
LG1867	5469	The restless sink in their beds—they fitfully sleep.
LG1867	5470	Now I pierce the darkness—new beings appear,
LG1867	5471	The earth recedes from me into the night,
LG1867	5472	I saw that it was beautiful, and I see that what is not the earth is beautiful.
LG1867	5473	I go from bedside to bedside—I sleep close with the other sleepers, each in turn,
LG1867	5474	I dream in my dream all the dreams of the other dreamers,
LG1867	5475	And I become the other dreamers.
LG1867	5476	I am a dance—Play up, there! the fit is whirling me fast!
LG1867	5477	I am the ever-laughing—it is new moon and twi-lighttwilight,
LG1867	5478	I see the hiding of douceurs—I see nimble ghosts whichever way I look,
LG1867	5479	Cache, and cache again, deep in the ground and sea,and where it is neither ground or sea.
LG1867	5480	Well do they do their jobs, those journeymen di-vinedivine,
LG1867	5481	Only from me can they hide nothing, and would not if they could,
LG1867	5482	I reckon I am their boss, and they make me a pet besides,
LG1867	5483	And surround me and lead me, and run ahead when I walk,
LG1867	5484	To lift their cunning covers, to signify me with stretch'd arms, and resume the way;
LG1867	5485	Onward we move! a gay gang of blackguards! with mirth-shouting music, and wild-flapping pen-nantspennants of joy!
LG1867	5486	I am the actor, the actress, the voter, the politician;
LG1867	5487	The emigrant and the exile, the criminal that stood in the box,
LG1867	5488	He who has been famous, and he who shall be famous after to-day,
LG1867	5489	The stammerer, the well-form'd person, the wasted or feeble person.
LG1867	5490	I am she who adorn'd herself and folded her hair expectantly,
LG1867	5491	My truant lover has come, and it is dark.
LG1867	5492	Double yourself and receive me, darkness!
LG1867	5493	Receive me and my lover too—he will not let me go without him.
LG1867	5494	I roll myself upon you, as upon a bed—I resign myself to the dusk.
LG1867	5495	He whom I call answers me, and takes the place of my lover,
LG1867	5496	He rises with me silently from the bed.
LG1867	5497	Darkness! you are gentler than my lover—his flesh was sweaty and panting,
LG1867	5498	I feel the hot moisture yet that he left me.
LG1867	5499	My hands are spread forth, I pass them in all direc-tionsdirections,
LG1867	5500	I would sound up the shadowy shore to which you are journeying.
LG1867	5501	Be careful, darkness! already, what was it touch'd me?
LG1867	5502	I thought my lover had gone, else darkness and he are one,
LG1867	5503	I hear the heart-beat—I follow, I fade away.
LG1867	5504	O hot-cheek'd and blushing! O foolish hectic!
LG1867	5505	O for pity's sake, no one must see me now! my clothes were stolen while I was abed,
LG1867	5506	Now I am thrust forth, where shall I run?
LG1867	5507	Pier that I saw dimly last night, when I look'd from the windows!
LG1867	5508	Pier out from the main, let me catch myself with you,and stay—I will not chafe you,
LG1867	5509	I feel ashamed to go naked about the world.
LG1867	5510	I am curious to know where my feet stand—and what this is flooding me, childhood or manhood —and the hunger that crosses the bridge between.
LG1867	5511	The cloth laps a first sweet eating and drinking,
LG1867	5512	Laps life-swelling yolks—laps ear of rose-corn, milky and just ripen'd;
LG1867	5513	The white teeth stay, and the boss-tooth advances in darkness,
LG1867	5514	And liquor is spill'd on lips and bosoms by touching glasses, and the best liquor afterward.
LG1867	5515	I descend my western course, my sinews are flaccid,
LG1867	5516	Perfume and youth course through me, and I am their wake.
LG1867	5517	It is my face yellow and wrinkled, instead of the old woman's,
LG1867	5518	I sit low in a straw-bottom chair, and carefully darn my grandson's stockings.
LG1867	5519	It is I too, the sleepless widow, looking out on the winter midnight,
LG1867	5520	I see the sparkles of starshine on the icy and pallid earth.
LG1867	5521	A shroud I see, and I am the shroud—I wrap a body,and lie in the coffin,
LG1867	5522	It is dark here under ground—it is not evil or pain here—it is blank here, for reasons.
LG1867	5523	It seems to me that everything in the light and air ought to be happy,
LG1867	5524	Whoever is not in his coffin and the dark grave, let him know he has enough.
LG1867	5525	I see a beautiful gigantic swimmer, swimming naked through the eddies of the sea,
LG1867	5526	His brown hair lies close and even to his head—he strikes out with courageous arms—he urges himself with his legs,
LG1867	5527	I see his white body—I see his undaunted eyes,
LG1867	5528	I hate the swift-running eddies that would dash him head-foremost on the rocks.
LG1867	5529	What are you doing, you ruffianly red-trickled waves?
LG1867	5530	Will you kill the courageous giant? Will you kill him in the prime of his middle age?
LG1867	5531	Steady and long he struggles,
LG1867	5532	He is baffled, bang'd, bruis'd—he holds out while his strength holds out,
LG1867	5533	The slapping eddies are spotted with his blood—they bear him away—they roll him, swing him, turn him,

LG1867	5555	Now I tell what my mother told me to-day as we sat at dinner together,
LG1867	5556	Of when she was a nearly grown girl, living home with her parents on the old homestead.
LG1867	5557	A red squaw came one breakfast-time to the old homestead,
LG1867	5558	On her back she carried a bundle of rushes for rush-bottomingrush-bottoming chairs,

LG1867	5561	My mother look'd in delight and amazement at the stranger,

LG1867	5563	The more she look'd upon her, she loved her,
LG1867	5564	Never before had she seen such wonderful beauty and purity,
LG1867	5565	She made her sit on a bench by the jamb of the fire-placefireplace—she cook'd food for her,

LG1867	5567	The red squaw staid all the forenoon, and toward the middle of the afternoon she went away,

LG1867	5569	All the week she thought of her—she watch'd for her many a month,
LG1867	5570	She remember'd her many a winter and many a summer,
LG1867	5571	But the squaw never came, nor was heard of there again.
LG1867	5572	Now Lucifer was not dead—or if he was, I am his sorrowful terrible heir;

LG1867	5641	I too pass from the night,
